---
title: "Brownian motion and the forward diffusion process"
description: "The noising half of a diffusion model: Brownian motion (the Wiener process) and its properties, the DDPM forward chain q(x_t | x_{t-1}) with its closed form q(x_t | x_0) and the reparameterization x_t = √ᾱ_t x_0 + √(1−ᾱ_t) ε, linear and cosine noise schedules, why the chain is variance preserving, and the continuous-time view as a stochastic differential equation (VP and VE SDEs), with numpy simulations."

aliases: ["Brownian motion", "Wiener process", "forward process", "noising process", "noise schedule", "cosine schedule", "VP SDE", "VE SDE", "variance preserving"]

*Beyond the lectures · [[8-diffusion-models/index|Diffusion models]] · sourced from Sohl-Dickstein et al. (2015), Ho et al. (2020), Nichol & Dhariwal (2021) and Song et al. (2021) · builds on Gaussian random variables and [[2-calculus-and-optimization/gradient-descent|step-by-step updates]]*

> [!abstract] In one breath
> A diffusion model learns to generate images by first learning what happens when you **destroy** them. The destruction is fixed and simple: add a little Gaussian noise, shrink a little, repeat — $x_t = \sqrt{1-\beta_t}\,x_{t-1} + \sqrt{\beta_t}\,\varepsilon_t$ — until after $T$ steps nothing but $\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I)$ noise is left. Because every step is Gaussian, any $x_t$ can be sampled from the clean image in **one** jump, $x_t = \sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0 + \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\varepsilon$, which is what makes training cheap. In the limit of many tiny steps the chain becomes a **stochastic differential equation** driven by **Brownian motion**, $\mathrm d x = -\tfrac12\beta(t)x\,\mathrm dt + \sqrt{\beta(t)}\,\mathrm d W$. Generation is this process run backwards, which is the subject of the [[8-diffusion-models/reverse-time-sde-and-score-matching|next page]].

## What it is

Sohl-Dickstein et al. (2015) put the idea in one sentence: "systematically and slowly destroy structure in a data distribution through an iterative forward diffusion process", then "learn a reverse diffusion process that restores structure in data". This page is about the first half, the **forward process**. It has no learnable parameters; it is a recipe for turning any image $x_0$ into noise, chosen so that the reverse direction can be learned.

> [!definition] Definition — the DDPM forward process (Ho et al., 2020, eq. 2)
> Given a **variance schedule** $\beta_1,\dots,\beta_T\in(0,1)$, the forward process is the Markov chain
> $$
> q(x_t \mid x_{t-1}) = \mathcal N\!\left(x_t;\ \sqrt{1-\beta_t}\,x_{t-1},\ \beta_t\mathbf I\right),\qquad t=1,\dots,T,
> $$
> started at a data point $x_0$ (an image with pixels scaled to $[-1,1]$). With $\alpha_t := 1-\beta_t$ and $\bar\alpha_t := \prod_{s=1}^{t}\alpha_s$, every intermediate step has the closed form (eq. 4)
> $$
> q(x_t \mid x_0) = \mathcal N\!\left(x_t;\ \sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0,\ (1-\bar\alpha_t)\mathbf I\right),\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad x_t = \sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0 + \sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\varepsilon,\ \ \varepsilon\sim\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I).
> $$

## What you should know

- The defining properties of Brownian motion $W(t)$: $W(0)=0$, independent increments, $W(t)-W(s)\sim\mathcal N(0,\,t-s)$, continuous but nowhere-differentiable paths; its spread grows like $\sqrt t$.
- The one-step forward kernel $q(x_t\mid x_{t-1})$ and why the mean is **shrunk** by $\sqrt{1-\beta_t}$: it keeps the variance at 1 (variance preserving).
- How to derive the closed form $q(x_t\mid x_0)$ by adding Gaussians, and the reparameterization $x_t=\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}x_0+\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\varepsilon$ that every training step uses.
- The two standard schedules: Ho et al.'s linear $\beta_t$ from $10^{-4}$ to $0.02$ over $T=1000$ steps, and Nichol & Dhariwal's cosine schedule $\bar\alpha_t = f(t)/f(0)$, $f(t)=\cos^2\!\big(\tfrac{t/T+s}{1+s}\cdot\tfrac\pi2\big)$, $s=0.008$; what $\bar\alpha_t$ and the signal-to-noise ratio $\bar\alpha_t/(1-\bar\alpha_t)$ mean.
- The continuous-time view: an Itô SDE $\mathrm dx = f(x,t)\,\mathrm dt + g(t)\,\mathrm dW$; the **VP SDE** $\mathrm dx=-\tfrac12\beta(t)x\,\mathrm dt+\sqrt{\beta(t)}\,\mathrm dW$ is the limit of the DDPM chain, the **VE SDE** $\mathrm dx=\sqrt{\mathrm d[\sigma^2(t)]/\mathrm dt}\,\mathrm dW$ the limit of the NCSN noise ladder.
- Why the forward process is chosen this way: it ends at a distribution you can sample ($\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I)$), it is cheap to sample at any $t$, and its small Gaussian steps make the reverse steps approximately Gaussian too.

## Brownian motion

In 1827 the botanist Robert Brown watched tiny particles from pollen grains jitter ceaselessly in water; Einstein explained the motion in 1905 as bombardment by the liquid's molecules, with a mean squared displacement growing linearly in time. The mathematical model of that jitter is **Brownian motion**, made rigorous by Norbert Wiener around 1920 and therefore also called the **Wiener process**.

> [!definition] Definition — standard Brownian motion (Wiener process)
> A random function $W(t)$, $t\ge 0$, with
> 1. $W(0)=0$;
> 2. **independent increments**: $W(t_1)-W(s_1)$ and $W(t_2)-W(s_2)$ are independent when the intervals $[s_1,t_1]$ and $[s_2,t_2]$ do not overlap;
> 3. **Gaussian increments**: $W(t)-W(s)\sim\mathcal N(0,\,t-s)$ for $s<t$;
> 4. **continuous paths**.
>
> In $d$ dimensions, $\mathbf W(t)$ has $d$ independent coordinates of this kind.

Three consequences matter here. The variance grows **linearly** in time, $\operatorname{Var}W(t)=t$, so a typical excursion is of size $\sqrt t$ — the band in the figure. Over a short step $\Delta t$ the increment is $\sqrt{\Delta t}\,z$ with $z\sim\mathcal N(0,1)$, which is how it is simulated. And the paths are so rough that, with probability one, they are differentiable **nowhere**: $\Delta W/\Delta t\sim 1/\sqrt{\Delta t}$ blows up as $\Delta t\to0$. That is why one writes $\mathrm dW$ in an equation and never $\mathrm dW/\mathrm dt$.

Brownian motion is also the limit of a coin-flip random walk: sum $n$ independent $\pm1$ steps up to time $t$, divide by $\sqrt n$, and as $n\to\infty$ the result converges to $W(t)$ (Donsker's theorem). Noise added in many small independent pieces is Brownian, whatever the pieces looked like.

height="166.3"/></clipPath></defs></svg> <figcaption><b>Brownian motion.</b> Left: five sample paths of a standard Wiener process on [0, 1], simulated as cumulative sums of 1,000 independent N(0, Δt) increments. The spread grows like √t (dashed: ±2√t); over 4,000 paths the sample variance at t = 1 is 0.990 and at t = 0.5 it is 0.500. Right: a ±1 coin-flip walk with its steps scaled by 1/√n already looks like a Brownian path; as n grows it converges to one (Donsker's theorem).</figcaption></figure>

```python
n, T = 1000, 1.0
dt = T / n
dW = np.sqrt(dt) * rng.standard_normal((5, n))   # independent N(0, dt) steps
W = np.concatenate([np.zeros((5, 1)), np.cumsum(dW, 1)], 1)
```

## The forward process, step by step

A diffusion model applies the same kind of noise to data, but with a **shrink** at every step so the result settles at a fixed distribution instead of spreading forever:

$$
x_t = \sqrt{1-\beta_t}\;x_{t-1} + \sqrt{\beta_t}\;\varepsilon_t,\qquad \varepsilon_t\sim\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I)\ \text{independent}.
$$

**Why $\sqrt{1-\beta_t}$?** If $x_{t-1}$ has (per-coordinate) variance 1, then $\operatorname{Var}x_t=(1-\beta_t)\cdot1+\beta_t=1$. The shrink exactly cancels the variance that the noise adds, so data scaled to unit-ish variance stays at that scale while its **structure** is replaced by noise. Ho et al. scale image pixels from $\{0,\dots,255\}$ to $[-1,1]$ for this reason (Sec. 3.3).

**The closed form.** Write $\alpha_t=1-\beta_t$ and unroll two steps:

$$
x_t=\sqrt{\alpha_t}\,x_{t-1}+\sqrt{1-\alpha_t}\,\varepsilon_t
=\sqrt{\alpha_t\alpha_{t-1}}\,x_{t-2}+\underbrace{\sqrt{\alpha_t(1-\alpha_{t-1})}\,\varepsilon_{t-1}+\sqrt{1-\alpha_t}\,\varepsilon_t}_{\text{sum of independent Gaussians}} .
$$

A sum of independent zero-mean Gaussians is Gaussian with the variances added: $\alpha_t(1-\alpha_{t-1})+(1-\alpha_t)=1-\alpha_t\alpha_{t-1}$. So $x_t=\sqrt{\alpha_t\alpha_{t-1}}\,x_{t-2}+\sqrt{1-\alpha_t\alpha_{t-1}}\,\bar\varepsilon$, and by induction down to $x_0$:

$$
x_t=\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}\,x_0+\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}\,\varepsilon,\qquad \bar\alpha_t=\prod_{s=1}^t(1-\beta_s).
$$

This is the single most-used formula in diffusion models. $\bar\alpha_t$ is the fraction of the variance that is still **signal**; $1-\bar\alpha_t$ is the noise variance. To make a training example at a random step $t$ you never run $t$ noising steps — you draw one $\varepsilon$ and evaluate this line ([[8-diffusion-models/ddpm|DDPM's Algorithm 1]]).

width="66.9" height="66.9"/></clipPath></defs></svg> <figcaption><b>The forward process on a 2-D toy dataset</b> (a ring of eight tight Gaussian blobs, 600 points), noised in one jump per panel with x_t = √ᾱ_t x_0 + √(1−ᾱ_t) ε and the linear schedule. The same ε is used in every panel, so each point drifts along a straight line from its data position toward its noise sample. By t = 1,000 nothing of the ring is left: the cloud is a standard normal.</figcaption></figure>

> [!example] A worked number
> With Ho et al.'s schedule, $\bar\alpha_{50}=0.971$: after 50 of 1,000 steps a pixel is $\sqrt{0.971}=0.985$ times its original value plus noise of standard deviation $\sqrt{0.029}=0.17$ — still clearly visible. At $t=1000$, $\bar\alpha_T=4.0\times10^{-5}$: the signal coefficient is $\sqrt{4.0\times10^{-5}}\approx0.0064$, so $x_T$ is noise for all practical purposes. Ho et al. report that the KL divergence between $q(x_T\mid x_0)$ and $\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I)$ is about $10^{-5}$ bits per dimension (Sec. 4).

## Noise schedules

The schedule $\beta_1,\dots,\beta_T$ — equivalently the curve $\bar\alpha_t$ — decides how fast information is destroyed.

| Schedule | Definition | Where |
| --- | --- | --- |
| linear | $\beta_t$ increasing linearly from $\beta_1=10^{-4}$ to $\beta_T=0.02$, $T=1000$ | Ho et al. (2020), Sec. 4 |
| cosine | $\bar\alpha_t=f(t)/f(0)$, $f(t)=\cos^2\!\big(\tfrac{t/T+s}{1+s}\cdot\tfrac\pi2\big)$, $s=0.008$; $\beta_t=1-\bar\alpha_t/\bar\alpha_{t-1}$ clipped to at most 0.999 | Nichol & Dhariwal (2021), eq. 17 |

Ho et al. chose their $\beta$'s "small relative to data scaled to $[-1,1]$" so that each reverse step has nearly the same Gaussian form as the forward one, while keeping the signal at $x_T$ as small as possible. Nichol & Dhariwal found that this linear schedule works for high-resolution images but destroys information too quickly at $64\times64$ and $32\times32$: the last part of the chain is so noisy that it barely matters — a model trained with it hardly gets worse when up to 20% of the reverse process is skipped. Their cosine schedule makes $\bar\alpha_t$ fall roughly linearly in the middle and change very little near $t=0$ and $t=T$; the offset $s=0.008$ keeps the first $\beta$'s from being too tiny (they chose it so $\sqrt{\beta_0}$ is slightly below the pixel bin width $1/127.5$), and the clip prevents a singularity at $t=T$.

stroke-linecap: square"/></g></g></g><defs><clipPath id="ffd7bd2"><rect x="46.6" y="7.2" width="192.7" height="166.3"/></clipPath><clipPath id="ffd7bd3"><rect x="278.0" y="7.2" width="192.7" height="166.3"/></clipPath></defs></svg> <figcaption><b>Two noise schedules over T = 1,000 steps.</b> Left: the surviving signal fraction ᾱ_t. The linear schedule (β from 0.0001 to 0.02) has ᾱ below 0.5 by step 260 and ᾱ_T = 4.0e-05; the cosine schedule falls slowly at both ends and crosses 0.5 at step 497. Right: signal-to-noise ratio on a log scale (SNR = 1 where signal and noise have equal variance). Computed from the formulas, not from a model.</figcaption></figure>

```python
def linear_betas(T=1000):
    return np.linspace(1e-4, 0.02, T)

def cosine_alpha_bar(T=1000, s=0.008):
    t = np.arange(T + 1)
    f = np.cos((t / T + s) / (1 + s) * np.pi / 2) ** 2
    ab = f / f[0]
    betas = np.minimum(1 - ab[1:] / ab[:-1], 0.999)   # clip near t = T
    return np.cumprod(1 - betas)

ab_lin = np.cumprod(1 - linear_betas())   # ᾱ_t, t = 1..1000
```

The **signal-to-noise ratio** $\mathrm{SNR}(t)=\bar\alpha_t/(1-\bar\alpha_t)$ is a schedule-independent way to say how noisy step $t$ is: SNR $=1$ means signal and noise have equal variance. In the numbers above, the linear schedule passes $\bar\alpha=0.5$ (SNR $=1$) at step 260 and the cosine one at step 497.

height="57.1"/></clipPath></defs></svg> <figcaption><b>Noising an image</b> (a 64×64 test image drawn in numpy, pixels scaled to [−1, 1]) with the closed form x_t = √ᾱ_t x_0 + √(1−ᾱ_t) ε, one fixed ε for every panel; values outside [−1, 1] are clipped for display. Top: linear schedule; bottom: cosine schedule. With the linear schedule the picture is essentially gone by t = 500; the cosine schedule keeps visible structure longer, which is what Nichol and Dhariwal designed it for.</figcaption></figure>

## The continuous-time view: a stochastic differential equation

Make the steps smaller and more numerous. Song et al. (2021, App. B) set $\beta_i=\beta(t)\,\Delta t$ with $\Delta t=1/N$ and $t=i/N$; for small $\Delta t$, $\sqrt{1-\beta(t)\Delta t}\approx1-\tfrac12\beta(t)\Delta t$, so one step of the chain reads

$$
x(t+\Delta t)\approx x(t)-\tfrac12\beta(t)\,x(t)\,\Delta t+\sqrt{\beta(t)}\,\underbrace{\sqrt{\Delta t}\,z}_{\approx\,\Delta W}.
$$

As $N\to\infty$ this becomes an **Itô stochastic differential equation**:

> [!definition] Definition — forward SDE (Song et al., 2021, eq. 5)
> $$
> \mathrm dx = f(x,t)\,\mathrm dt + g(t)\,\mathrm dW,
> $$
> where $W$ is a standard Brownian motion, $f$ is the **drift** (a deterministic push) and $g$ the **diffusion coefficient** (how much Brownian noise is injected). Read it as the limit of $x\leftarrow x+f(x,t)\Delta t+g(t)\sqrt{\Delta t}\,z$ — exactly the **Euler–Maruyama** simulation rule.

Two choices of $f$ and $g$ reproduce the two families of earlier models (Sec. 3.4):

| SDE | $f(x,t)$ | $g(t)$ | Discrete version | Distribution of $x(t)$ given $x(0)$ |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **VP** (variance preserving) | $-\tfrac12\beta(t)\,x$ | $\sqrt{\beta(t)}$ | DDPM chain, $x_i=\sqrt{1-\beta_i}x_{i-1}+\sqrt{\beta_i}z$ | $\mathcal N\!\big(x(0)e^{-\frac12\int_0^t\beta},\ (1-e^{-\int_0^t\beta})\mathbf I\big)$ |
| **VE** (variance exploding) | $0$ | $\sqrt{\mathrm d[\sigma^2(t)]/\mathrm dt}$ | NCSN noise ladder, $x_i=x_{i-1}+\sqrt{\sigma_i^2-\sigma_{i-1}^2}\,z$ | $\mathcal N\!\big(x(0),\ [\sigma^2(t)-\sigma^2(0)]\mathbf I\big)$ |

The VE SDE is pure (time-changed) Brownian motion started at the data: the variance grows without bound, so it needs a large final $\sigma_{\max}$. The VP SDE is Brownian motion with a pull toward 0 — an Ornstein–Uhlenbeck-type process — whose covariance obeys $\Sigma(t)=\mathbf I+e^{-\int_0^t\beta}(\Sigma(0)-\mathbf I)$: it stays at $\mathbf I$ if it starts there, hence the name. Song et al. use $\beta(t)=0.1+t\,(20-0.1)$ on $t\in[0,1]$ "to match the settings in Ho et al.": with $\Delta t=1/1000$ that is $\beta_i$ from $0.1/1000=10^{-4}$ to $20/1000=0.02$. As a check, the continuous signal fraction at $t=1$ is $e^{-\int_0^1\beta}=e^{-10.05}=4.3\times10^{-5}$, next to the discrete chain's $\bar\alpha_{1000}=4.0\times10^{-5}$.

167.9  " style="fill: none; stroke: currentColor; stroke-width: 0.8; stroke-linejoin: miter; stroke-linecap: square"/></g></g></g><defs><clipPath id="f327752"><rect x="45.4" y="7.2" width="368.2" height="160.7"/></clipPath></defs></svg> <figcaption><b>The VP SDE moving a 1-D distribution to N(0, 1).</b> Grey shading: the exact density p_t(x) of a two-component mixture (weights 0.3 and 0.7) under dx = −½β(t)x dt + √β(t) dW with β(t) = 0.1 + 19.9t. Blue: six forward paths simulated with Euler–Maruyama. Each path is a Brownian motion with a pull toward 0; together they turn two bumps into one standard normal.</figcaption></figure>

```python
B_MIN, B_MAX = 0.1, 20.0
def beta(t):
    return B_MIN + t * (B_MAX - B_MIN)

for i in range(n):                      # Euler–Maruyama, forward in time
    t, dt = tt[i], tt[i + 1] - tt[i]
    x = x - 0.5 * beta(t) * x * dt + np.sqrt(beta(t) * dt) * rng.standard_normal(x.shape)
```

Why bother with the continuous view? Because it separates the **model** (the SDE, i.e. the family of distributions $p_t$) from the **discretization** used to sample it. Once the process is an SDE, results from stochastic calculus apply — above all Anderson's theorem that a diffusion run backwards in time is again a diffusion, with a drift that depends on the **score** $\nabla_x\log p_t(x)$. That is the [[8-diffusion-models/reverse-time-sde-and-score-matching|reverse-time SDE]], and it is what the model learns.

> [!intuition] What the forward process buys
> - **A known endpoint.** Whatever the data, $x_T\approx\mathcal N(0,\mathbf I)$, which is trivial to sample: generation can start there.
> - **Free training pairs.** For any image and any $t$, $(x_t,\varepsilon)$ costs one random draw. The network is trained to undo noise it can see being made.
> - **Small, Gaussian steps.** When each $\beta_t$ is small, the true reverse step $q(x_{t-1}\mid x_t)$ is also close to Gaussian (Sohl-Dickstein et al.), so a network only has to predict a mean.
> - **No parameters.** Unlike a VAE encoder, $q$ is fixed; all learning happens in the reverse direction.

> [!trap] Common mistakes
> - **Forgetting the square roots.** The mean coefficient is $\sqrt{\bar\alpha_t}$ and the noise **standard deviation** is $\sqrt{1-\bar\alpha_t}$; $\bar\alpha_t$ and $1-\bar\alpha_t$ are variances.
> - **$\bar\alpha_t$ vs $\alpha_t$.** $\alpha_t=1-\beta_t$ is one step; $\bar\alpha_t$ is the product of all steps up to $t$. Papers differ: Song et al. (2021) write $\alpha_i$ for what Ho et al. call $\bar\alpha_i$, and Improved DDPM starts its product at $s=0$.
> - **Adding noise without shrinking.** $x_t=x_{t-1}+\sqrt{\beta_t}\varepsilon$ is the VE construction; the DDPM chain shrinks too. Mixing the two gives the wrong closed form.
> - **Thinking Brownian increments scale with $\Delta t$.** They scale with $\sqrt{\Delta t}$; simulating with `dt * z` instead of `np.sqrt(dt) * z` makes the noise vanish as steps shrink.
> - **Unscaled data.** The schedules assume data in $[-1,1]$ (or unit variance). Feeding pixels in $[0,255]$ leaves a large signal at $t=T$.

> [!question]- Check yourself
> 1. With $\beta_1=\beta_2=0.1$, write $q(x_2\mid x_0)$.
> 2. Why does the DDPM chain multiply $x_{t-1}$ by $\sqrt{1-\beta_t}$ instead of leaving it alone?
> 3. A Brownian motion is simulated with steps of $\Delta t=0.01$. What is the standard deviation of one increment, and of $W(1)$?
> 4. Which forward SDE has $f=0$, and what happens to its variance?
>
> > [!solution]- Answers
> > 1. $\bar\alpha_2=0.9\cdot0.9=0.81$, so $q(x_2\mid x_0)=\mathcal N(0.9\,x_0,\ 0.19\,\mathbf I)$.
> > 2. To keep the total variance fixed: $(1-\beta)\cdot1+\beta=1$. Without the shrink the variance would grow by $\beta_t$ every step (the VE construction).
> > 3. $\sqrt{0.01}=0.1$ per increment; $W(1)$ has variance 1, so standard deviation 1 (100 independent increments of variance 0.01).
> > 4. The VE SDE: $x(t)=x(0)+$ Brownian noise of variance $\sigma^2(t)-\sigma^2(0)$, which grows without bound.

## References

- Sohl-Dickstein, Weiss, Maheswaranathan, Ganguli (2015). *Deep Unsupervised Learning using Nonequilibrium Thermodynamics.* [arXiv:1503.03585](https://arxiv.org/abs/1503.03585)
- Ho, Jain, Abbeel (2020). *Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models.* [arXiv:2006.11239](https://arxiv.org/abs/2006.11239) — eqs. 2 and 4, Sec. 3.3 (data scaling), Sec. 4 (schedule, $T=1000$).
- Nichol, Dhariwal (2021). *Improved Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models.* [arXiv:2102.09672](https://arxiv.org/abs/2102.09672) — Sec. 3.2, eq. 17 (cosine schedule).
- Song, Sohl-Dickstein, Kingma, Kumar, Ermon, Poole (2021). *Score-Based Generative Modeling through Stochastic Differential Equations.* [arXiv:2011.13456](https://arxiv.org/abs/2011.13456) — Sec. 3.1, 3.4, App. B (VE/VP SDEs, perturbation kernels) and App. C ($\bar\beta_{\min}=0.1$, $\bar\beta_{\max}=20$).
- Song, Ermon (2019). *Generative Modeling by Estimating Gradients of the Data Distribution.* [arXiv:1907.05600](https://arxiv.org/abs/1907.05600) — the noise ladder behind the VE SDE.
- Brownian motion: APS News, [August 1827: Robert Brown and molecular motion](https://www.aps.org/apsnews/2016/08/robert-brown-molecular-motion-pollen) (history); S. Lalley, [lecture notes on the Wiener process](http://galton.uchicago.edu/~lalley/Courses/313/WienerProcess.pdf) (definition, nowhere differentiability); [Wiener process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wiener_process) (definition, Donsker's theorem).
